Let me think about what distinguishes SPLIT from SILENT in the mark rules. SPLIT: "the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open." SILENT: "the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

The difference: in SPLIT, the theory's text itself is ambiguous — two readings of the theory's sentences give different findings, and the theory doesn't resolve which reading is right. In SILENT, the theory's sentences are clear but depend on an input (a declared value/condition) that the case doesn't provide — so the theory can't compute a verdict.

For O35 under file 10: is the theory's text ambiguous about what r says, or does the theory take r's content as a fixed-for-the-comparison input that the case leaves unstated? (P) is evaluated on "claimed obligations O and protected obligations P, fixed for the comparison" — i.e., the comparison (the application to the case) fixes O and P. The case doesn't state them. So the deciding matter (the content of P) is "an input the case leaves unstated" → SILENT (rule 4). The "two readings" are not two readings of the theory's sentences; they're two possible inputs. The theory's sentences don't support two readings of what the theory says; the theory says "whatever P is, fixed for the comparison". So rule 4 fits and rule 3 doesn't (the theory's sentences don't support two readings — there's one reading of the theory, and the input is unfilled). Hmm, but one could say the theory's sentences support both r=state-condition and r=history-condition as "readings". But those aren't readings of the theory; they're values of an input variable.

The obligations are declared inputs: \(O\) says what is to be repaired and \(P\) what is to be protected, each as a stated condition over stated occasions, and a protected condition is lost exactly when it fails on an occasion it covers. A correct account that produced nothing, an act that repaired without an account, and a repair produced through use of an account are three different attributions."

The key contested point: does file 10 reach "Neither can say 'mostly'" (AGREE) or leave it to an input (SILENT)?

The ruling's reasoning: (1) the point is about what either party can assert ("Neither can **say** 'mostly'"); (2) a verdict that says a claim cannot be made is reached when the input that would warrant the claim is absent (O40 is of the first kind — "cannot be said" — vs O21/O27/O50 which assert "mostly X" and are SILENT); (3) under file 10, "a ranking of thinkers" is among the things the theory does not supply, and it marks those places empty (f10 L25); the case supplies no ranking; with the place empty, neither party has a ground for "mostly"; the theory's verdict is the fixed verdict with the qualification "absent a ranking", which counts as the same finding (Same finding rule); (4) f11 L27 s2 treats "a ranking of thinkers" as a declared input; f11 L27 s3 ("It does not supply a division of credit among contributors beyond what a history establishes") has the same form as f10 L25's "does not supply"; neither file bars a declared ranking; both leave the place empty in this case → same route → verdict SAME; (5) "theirs together" is reached in both files: robot's noticing = robot's construction (f10 L414), expert's fix = expert's binding, relayed; under file 11, the expert's proposal "remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" (f11 L419 s2) and ProducedBy "credits each contribution the history establishes" (f11 L433 s4).

Contrary readings: (i) SILENT under file 10 (the text lens, 04c): f10 L25 "does not say that a ranking cannot be had. It says: 'It supplies places where such things would go if anyone had them, and marks those places as empty.' A 'mostly' between two contributors is a ranking. On file 10, whether either can say it is decided by what fills that place, and the case supplies nothing." → mark rule 4 (input the case leaves unstated) → SILENT before AGREE. S72 read file 10 so. 1C B's own SILENT contrary "HOLDS: YES". (ii) the third reading: file 10 SILENT and file 11 AGREE → SILENT→AGREE, a theory change toward (through f11 L27 s3 and L433 s4 binding "mostly" to the history rather than an input place). (iii) the robot's credit reading: treat the expert's fix as an incoming carrier in the robot's construction → the reinterpretation is the robot's (rejected on f11 L419 s2 and f10 L414).

What turns on it: no reading gives a change away; it decides only whether O40 is an S75 correction or a theory change toward; it bears on P2's scoring.

**Part 2 — O40**
1. The live question: the file-10 mark turns on the text (the rule corrects the baseline only if both 1Ks agree AND the determiner's reading confirms). The point is claim-availability: "Neither can say 'mostly'", with "say" and the quoted "mostly" marking the claim someone would make; it is not the share-finding "neither has most" — the preceding sentence ("The reinterpretation was theirs together") already carries the division, and the third sentence draws the claim-consequence. On the family's phrasings ("mostly the expert's" — plain share assertions in O21, O27, O50) the contrast is deliberate.
2. On that reading, file 10 reaches the finding: the theory establishes both contributions (f10 L414: construction witnesses; "relay is not" keeps the expert's fix the expert's) and establishes that no weighing basis is in play (f10 L25: "does not supply... a merit function, or a ranking of thinkers... marks those places as empty"); the case's record supplies none. A "mostly" claim needs a warrant; there is none on the record; so neither can make it. The theory's verdict is the finding with the limit "absent a ranking" — the Same-finding rule counts a limit as the same finding.
3. The SILENT reading fails: mark rule 4 needs the deciding matter to be "an input the case leaves unstated". For a claim-availability finding the deciding matter is whether the record warrants the claim — and the record's warrant-content is settled (it has no weights). The 04c sentence concedes the point in its own words: "whether either can say it is decided by what fills that place, and the case supplies nothing" — with the place empty, nothing fills it, and the claim fails. "If anyone had them" speaks to other applications, not to hidden facts in this case.
4. The consistency test (why the point cannot be a share-finding): if the theory's verdict on O21/O27/O50 ("mostly X") is SILENT in both files — the determination so rules, and nothing here contradicts it — then the theory's position is "no division of credit is established". That position leaves share-findings open (SILENT) while delivering claim-availability findings (no established majority ⟹ no claimable majority). Reading (iv) as a share-finding instead would force either O21-type DISAGREEs (if the theory denies a majority) or a SILENT/SILENT package that makes f11's history clause (L27 s3, L433 s4) do nothing — against its words ("both are credited and the history supplies no division of credit that it does not contain").
5. File 11's mark: AGREE, via f11 L419 s2 ("Work supplied from outside that boundary... remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside") + L433 s4 (both credited; no division beyond the history) + L27 s3. The robot-credit contrary fails on L419 s2 and f10 L414's "relay is not".
6. No reading moves O40 away: AGREE/AGREE (this ruling), SILENT/SILENT, or SILENT/AGREE (toward). The third reading (file 10 SILENT, file 11 AGREE) would rest on reading the point as a share-finding while letting file 11's history clause settle it — an asymmetric treatment of one point (file 11's clause is about the credit the history supplies, i.e., claim-grounding, not share-truth). And it would not change (a)-(d) or the standing verdict: only the record (S75 correction vs theory change toward).

**Part 1 — O35**

1. *What the rule fixes.* The plan's file-10 rule is a rule about the counted mark: S75's baseline (SILENT) stands "unless both 1K returns agree on a different mark and [the determiner's] own reading of file 10 confirms it". Both 1K returns are SILENT. So the counted file-10 mark is SILENT under the rules however the text reads, and the row can fall only through the file-11 mark or a comparison element. The text question the determination leaves open is live all the same, because the04 section7.9 note says01's pre-registered reading (AGREE) would have made O35 a theory change away and failed (b) — so I test the text first.

2. *The endpoint reading of file 10 (01's AGREE) fails.* On it, (P) compares r at ξ and ξ' only, the tap runs at both, so P is preserved and the stop is "no loss of P". Against this: (P) is parametric in the content of its obligations — they are "fixed for the comparison" (f10 L438 = f11 L427) — and the case states no condition at all: the situation says "the protected tap", and nothing says what protection requires or over which occasions. The formula fixes when r is evaluated, not what r says. File 10 itself admits history-valued conditions: "Values of ports may be paths, functions, fields, proofs, or histories" (f10 L122 = f11 L107). On r = "the tap has run throughout the work", r(ξ') is false at ξ' although the tap runs at ξ', and (P) finds a loss with endpoints alone. O38's ruling (the same formula finds "it was broken, for four seconds" when the job sheet states such a condition) confirms that the content decides and the formula does not. Finally the verdict's own standard, "in every way that mattered", is a weighing the theory marks empty (f10 L25). Mark rule 4's first branch fits exactly: the deciding matter (the content and occasions of P) is an input the case leaves unstated.

3. *SPLIT does not fit.* The two candidate contents are two fillings of one unfilled input, not two readings of the theory's sentences: the theory says one thing (evaluate whatever P is, "fixed for the comparison"), and the case supplies no P. Rule 3 needs "the theory's sentences [to] support two readings"; here they support one and defer. Even if SPLIT were marked, it ranks with SILENT in direction and, both marks being non-AGREE on the same point ("held" and "not lost" are one fact under f11 L433 s1: "a protected condition is lost exactly when it fails on an occasion it covers"), the verdict would stay SAME — but the mark rules give SILENT.

4. *File 11's SILENT holds and is on the same point.* f11 L433 s1 makes the occasions part of the declared input ("each as a stated condition over stated occasions") and f11 L514 supplies the missing-input rule: "where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted." Both contrary file-11 readings fill the input from the verdict wanted: the occasions-as-draws reading gives the thoughtful person's finding; the blind DISAGREE (condition = "the tap runs", occasions = the work) takes its content from the same silence. L514 forbids both. The SILENTs concern the same point: under L433 s1 "held" and "not lost" are one fact, and in file 10 both turn on the content of P.

5. *The comparison elements.* Verdict SAME (two SILENTs, one point). Passage CHANGED (additive) is right: the file-11 verdict rests on f11 L433 s1 and L514, both ruled CLAIM (M38, M48) with no file-10 counterpart (section 4's list). Direction and kind unchanged. (For completeness: I also checked whether the verdict could rest on the shared sentence f10 L438 = f11 L427 and so make the passage SAME; it cannot, because file 11's SILENT is carried by the occasions clause and the unsettled-input rule, which are exactly the CLAIM places.)

**Part 2 — O40**

1. *The point is claim-availability.* "Neither can say 'mostly'." The word "say" and the quotation marks around "mostly" mark the claim a party would make ("mostly mine"), not a share-finding ("neither has most"): the preceding sentence already states the division ("The reinterpretation was theirs together"), and the third sentence draws its consequence for what either can claim. The case family confirms the contrast: O21, O27 and O50 assert shares in plain form ("mostly the expert's", "mostly the robot's"). The determination's reason 1 is right.

2. *On that reading file 10 reaches the finding.* A "mostly" claim needs a warrant: an established preponderance. File 10 establishes the two contributions — f10 L414's construction witnesses, with "relay is not" keeping the expert's fix the expert's rather than the robot's — and establishes that no weighing is in play: "It does not supply... a merit function, or a ranking of thinkers. It supplies places where such things would go if anyone had them, and marks those places as empty" (f10 L25). The case's record has the two seeings and no weights. So on the record neither party has a ground for "mostly", and the theory's verdict is the fixed finding with the limit "absent a ranking". The Same finding rule counts a limit as the same finding.

3. *The SILENT contrary fails.* Mark rule 4 needs "the deciding matter [as] an input the case leaves unstated". For a claim-availability finding the deciding matter is what the record warrants — and that is settled by the record (it has no weights); an unrecorded convention cannot ground a claim anyone can make. The 04c text-lens argument concedes this in its own words: "whether either can say it is decided by what fills that place, and the case supplies nothing." With the place empty in this application, the claim fails. "If anyone had them" describes other applications in which someone supplies a ranking; it does not posit an unstated ranking in this one.

4. *The consistency test — why the point cannot be a share-finding.* The determination rules O21, O27 and O50 SILENT in both files. Taken with f11 L433 s4 ("both are credited and the history supplies no division of credit that it does not contain") and L27 s3, this shows the theory's position on the division of credit is "no division is established here". That position leaves a share-finding open (SILENT on "mostly the expert's") and delivers a claim-availability finding (no established majority, hence no claimable majority) — the two marks the determination rules. If (iv) were instead a share-finding, the same theory position would leave O40 open too and make the determination's O21 and O40 rulings inconsistent... hmm — hmm, careful: the SILENT/SILENT package IS consistent with O21. Let me re-word: If (iv) were a share-finding, two packages follow, and each costs more than it buys. (a) SILENT in both files: then f11's history clauses say nothing that reaches any verdict in O40 — but "both are credited and the history supplies no division of credit that it does not contain" is a verdict about the credit the history supplies, and it reaches "theirs together" and "no majority claim". (b) SILENT under file 10 and AGREE under file 11 (the third reading): it needs file 11's clause to settle share-truth while file 10 merely abstains — an asymmetric reading of one point across the two files. On the claim-availability reading both files reach the finding by their own routes (file 10 through f10 L25's empty place; file 11 through L27 s3 and L433 s4), which is also what makes the verdict SAME and the passage CHANGED (additive) — the ruling's own comparison.

5. *File 11's AGREE.* f11 L419 s2: "Work supplied from outside that boundary, a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read, remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" — so the expert's proposal stays the expert's; and f11 L433 s4 credits both and supplies no division the history lacks. The robot-credit contrary (the expert's fix as an incoming carrier in the robot's witness) fails on f11 L419 s2 and f10 L414 ("relay is not").

6. *No reading moves the row away.* AGREE/AGREE (this ruling), SILENT/SILENT, or SILENT/AGREE (toward). None makes the file-11 mark further from AGREE than the file-10 mark, so (b) and the standing verdict are untouched whatever happens here; the row decides only S75 correction vs theory change toward.
